```bash
az network vnet peering delete -g rg --vnet-name vnet-A -n A-to-B
```

```powershell
Add-AzVirtualNetworkPeering -Name A-to-B -VirtualNetwork $vnetA -RemoteVirtualNetworkId $vnetB.Id `
  -AllowForwardedTraffic -AllowGatewayTransit
Add-AzVirtualNetworkPeering -Name B-to-A -VirtualNetwork $vnetB -RemoteVirtualNetworkId $vnetA.Id `
  -AllowForwardedTraffic -UseRemoteGateways

Get-AzVirtualNetworkPeering -VirtualNetworkName vnet-A -ResourceGroupName rg | Select Name, PeeringState
```

⚠️ **Trap:** Peering state stuck at **`Initiated`** → **the other side hasn't been configured.** `Connected` requires both.

---

## 5.6 User-Defined Routes and Routing

### Key Terms

| Term | Definition |
|---|---|
| **System routes** | Default routes Azure creates automatically. Cannot be deleted. |
| **UDR (User-Defined Route)** | A custom route you create in a **route table**. |
| **Route table** | A resource holding routes; **associated with SUBNETS** (never NICs). |
| **Address prefix** | The destination CIDR. |
| **Next hop type** | `VirtualNetworkGateway`, **`VirtualAppliance`**, `VirtualNetwork`, `Internet`, **`None`** (drop). |
| **Next hop IP address** | Required **only** for `VirtualAppliance`. |
| **BGP route** | A route learned dynamically over VPN/ExpressRoute. |
| **Effective routes** | 📌 The final computed route table on a NIC — the troubleshooting view. |
| **IP forwarding** | 📌 A **NIC-level setting** that must be **enabled on an NVA** so it can receive/forward packets not addressed to it. |
| **Forced tunneling** | Sending all internet traffic (`0.0.0.0/0`) to on-prem or a firewall for inspection. |
| **BGP** | Border Gateway Protocol — dynamic routing over VPN/ER. |
| **Longest prefix match** | More specific routes win: `/32` beats `/24` beats `/0`. |

### 📌 Default system routes

| Destination | Next hop |
|---|---|
| VNet address space(s) | `VirtualNetwork` |
| `0.0.0.0/0` | `Internet` |
| `10.0.0.0/8` | ⚠️ `None` (**dropped**) |
| `172.16.0.0/12` | ⚠️ `None` (**dropped**) |
| `192.168.0.0/16` | ⚠️ `None` (**dropped**) |
| `100.64.0.0/10` | ⚠️ `None` (**dropped**) |
| Peered VNet ranges | `VNetPeering` (auto-added) |
| On-prem ranges (with a gateway) | `VirtualNetworkGateway` |

📌 The RFC1918 → `None` routes exist so that traffic to private ranges you **haven't** defined gets dropped instead of leaking to the internet.

### 📌 Route selection order — memorize

```
1. USER-DEFINED ROUTE  (UDR)        ← highest priority
2. BGP route (VPN / ExpressRoute)
3. SYSTEM route                     ← lowest priority
```

**Then, among routes of the same type: LONGEST PREFIX MATCH wins.**

⚠️ **Trap:** "A UDR for `0.0.0.0/0` → firewall, and a BGP route for `0.0.0.0/0` from on-prem. Which wins?" → **The UDR.** UDR always beats BGP.
⚠️ **Trap:** "A UDR for `10.0.0.0/8` → NVA, and a system route for `10.0.1.0/24` (the VNet). Which wins for `10.0.1.5`?" → 📌 **The system route** — `/24` is **more specific** than `/8`. Longest prefix wins **within** the tie-break, but note the ordering rule applies only for **identical prefixes**.

📌 **The precise rule:** Azure picks by **longest prefix match first**. If two routes have the **same prefix**, then **UDR > BGP > System**.

### 📌 Next hop types

| Next hop | Meaning | Needs an IP? |
|---|---|---|
| **`VirtualNetworkGateway`** | Send to the VPN/ER gateway | ❌ |
| **`VirtualAppliance`** | Send to an NVA/firewall | ✅ **Yes — the NVA's private IP** |
| **`VirtualNetwork`** | Route within the VNet | ❌ |
| **`Internet`** | Send to the internet (via Azure's edge) | ❌ |
| **`None`** | 📌 **Drop the packet (blackhole)** | ❌ |

### 📌 IP forwarding — the NVA requirement

For an NVA (firewall, router, proxy VM) to forward traffic:

1. **Enable IP forwarding on the NVA's NIC** (Azure-level setting).
2. **Enable IP forwarding in the guest OS** (`net.ipv4.ip_forward=1` on Linux; `IPEnableRouter` registry on Windows).
3. Create a **UDR** pointing at the NVA's private IP.

⚠️ **THE NVA trap:** "The UDR points to the NVA but traffic is dropped." → **IP forwarding is not enabled on the NIC** (or in the guest OS). Azure silently drops packets a NIC receives that aren't addressed to it unless IP forwarding is on.

```bash
az network nic update -g rg -n nic-nva --ip-forwarding true
```

### 📌 Forced tunneling

Route all internet-bound traffic through an on-prem firewall or Azure Firewall:

```bash
az network route-table create -g rg -n rt-forced --disable-bgp-route-propagation false

az network route-table route create -g rg --route-table-name rt-forced \
  -n default-to-onprem --address-prefix 0.0.0.0/0 \
  --next-hop-type VirtualNetworkGateway

az network vnet subnet update -g rg --vnet-name vnet1 -n web --route-table rt-forced
```

⚠️ **Trap:** Never apply forced tunneling to `GatewaySubnet` — it breaks the gateway.
⚠️ **Trap:** `--disable-bgp-route-propagation true` stops the gateway's BGP routes from entering the subnet. Useful with an NVA hub; harmful if you need on-prem routes.

### 📌 Blackholing with `None`

```bash
# Block a specific range entirely
az network route-table route create -g rg --route-table-name rt-web \
  -n block-badnet --address-prefix 203.0.113.0/24 --next-hop-type None
```
💡 `None` is the answer for "drop traffic to X without an NSG."

### Commands

```bash
az network route-table create -g rg -n rt-spoke

az network route-table route create -g rg --route-table-name rt-spoke \
  -n to-firewall --address-prefix 0.0.0.0/0 \
  --next-hop-type VirtualAppliance --next-hop-ip-address 10.0.254.4

az network route-table route create -g rg --route-table-name rt-spoke \
  -n to-spoke-b --address-prefix 10.2.0.0/16 \
  --next-hop-type VirtualAppliance --next-hop-ip-address 10.0.254.4

az network vnet subnet update -g rg --vnet-name vnet-spoke-a -n web --route-table rt-spoke

az network route-table route list -g rg --route-table-name rt-spoke -o table

# ⚠️ THE troubleshooting command
az network nic show-effective-route-table -g rg -n nic-web-01 -o table
```

```powershell
$rt = New-AzRouteTable -ResourceGroupName rg -Name rt-spoke -Location eastus
Add-AzRouteConfig -RouteTable $rt -Name to-firewall -AddressPrefix 0.0.0.0/0 `
  -NextHopType VirtualAppliance -NextHopIpAddress 10.0.254.4
$rt | Set-AzRouteTable

Get-AzEffectiveRouteTable -NetworkInterfaceName nic-web-01 -ResourceGroupName rg | Format-Table
```

### 📌 Route table facts

| Fact | Value |
|---|---|
| Routes per route table | **400** |
| Route tables per subscription | 200 |
| Route tables per **subnet** | **1** |
| Can a route table attach to a NIC? | ❌ **No — subnets only** |
| Can one route table serve many subnets? | ✅ Yes (same region + subscription) |
| Region constraint | Route table and subnet must be in the **same region** |

---

## 5.7 Azure DNS

### Key Terms

| Term | Definition |
|---|---|
| **Azure DNS (public zone)** | Hosts **public** DNS records for a domain you own. Azure does **not** sell domains — use App Service Domains or an external registrar. |
| **Private DNS zone** | Internal name resolution within/between VNets, without a custom DNS server. |
| **Virtual network link** | Links a private DNS zone to a VNet so that VNet can **resolve** the zone. |
| **Auto-registration** | 📌 A link option that automatically creates/updates **A records** for VMs in the linked VNet. **Only one auto-registration link per VNet.** |
| **Registration VNet** | The VNet whose VMs auto-register. |
| **Resolution VNet** | A linked VNet that can resolve but does not auto-register. |
| **Name server (NS)** | The authoritative servers Azure assigns to a public zone; you set these at your **registrar** to delegate. |
| **SOA** | Start of Authority record — created automatically, one per zone. |
| **Record set** | All records with the same name and type. |
| **TTL** | Time To Live — how long resolvers cache the answer. |
| **Alias record** | 📌 An A/AAAA/CNAME record that **points at an Azure resource** (Public IP, Traffic Manager, Front Door, CDN, or another record set) and **updates automatically**. Supports the **zone apex**. |
| **Azure-provided DNS** | `168.63.129.16` — the default resolver for VMs. |
| **Custom DNS** | Point the VNet at your own DNS servers (VM-based DC, etc.). |
| **Azure DNS Private Resolver** | 📌 Managed service for **conditional forwarding** between on-prem DNS and Azure private zones. Replaces DNS forwarder VMs. |
| **Inbound endpoint** | Private Resolver endpoint that lets **on-prem** query Azure private zones. |
| **Outbound endpoint** | Private Resolver endpoint that forwards Azure queries **to on-prem**. |
| **DNS forwarding ruleset** | Rules mapping domains → target on-prem DNS servers. |

### 📌 DNS record types

| Type | Maps | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **A** | Name → **IPv4** | `www` → `203.0.113.5` |
| **AAAA** | Name → **IPv6** | `www` → `2001:db8::1` |
| **CNAME** | Name → **another name** | `www` → `app.azurewebsites.net` |
| **MX** | Mail exchange | `@` → `mail.contoso.com` (priority 10) |
| **TXT** | Arbitrary text (SPF, DKIM, **domain verification**) | `@` → `v=spf1 ...` |
| **NS** | Delegate a subdomain | `sub` → `ns1-01.azure-dns.com` |
| **SOA** | Zone authority | auto-created |
| **SRV** | Service location | `_sip._tcp` → host:port |
| **PTR** | IP → name (reverse) | |
| **CAA** | Which CAs may issue certs | |

⚠️ **THE CNAME trap:** 📌 **A CNAME cannot exist at the zone apex** (`contoso.com` with no subdomain) — it's a DNS protocol rule, because the apex must also hold SOA and NS records. 📌 **Azure's answer: use an ALIAS record** (an A record pointing at an Azure resource).

⚠️ **Trap:** "Point `contoso.com` (apex) at a public IP that might change." → **Alias record (type A)** targeting the Public IP resource. Not a CNAME.

### 📌 Alias records

| Alias A/AAAA can target | Alias CNAME can target |
|---|---|
| **Public IP** resource | Another **CNAME record set** |
| **Traffic Manager** profile (must have external endpoints) | |
| **Front Door / CDN** endpoint | |
| Another **A/AAAA record set** in the same zone | |

**Benefits:**
- Works at the **zone apex** ✅
- **Auto-updates** when the target's IP changes ✅
- **Auto-deletes** dangling records when the target is deleted → prevents **subdomain takeover** ✅
- Free ✅

### 📌 Public zone delegation

```
1. Create the zone in Azure DNS:  az network dns zone create -g rg -n contoso.com
2. Azure assigns 4 name servers:  ns1-01.azure-dns.com, ns2-01.azure-dns.net,
                                  ns3-01.azure-dns.org, ns4-01.azure-dns.info
3. Go to your DOMAIN REGISTRAR (GoDaddy, Namecheap, etc.)
4. Replace the registrar's NS records with Azure's 4 name servers
5. Wait for propagation (up to 48 h, usually much less)
```

⚠️ **Trap:** "Records were created in Azure DNS but nothing resolves." → **NS delegation at the registrar hasn't been done.** Azure DNS is not authoritative until the registrar points at it.
⚠️ **Trap:** Azure DNS does **not register domains**. You need a registrar (or App Service Domains).

### 📌 Private DNS zones

| Fact | Value |
|---|---|
| Scope | **Global** resource (not regional) |
| Zones per subscription | 1,000 |
| Record sets per zone | 25,000 |
| VNet links per zone | 1,000 |
| **VNet links with auto-registration per zone** | **100** |
| **Auto-registration links per VNET** | 📌 **1** — a VNet can auto-register into only **one** private zone |
| Works across regions? | ✅ Yes |
| Works across subscriptions? | ✅ Yes |
| Requires peering? | ❌ **No — a link is enough** |
| Split-horizon | ✅ You can have a private zone with the same name as a public zone |

⚠️ **THE private DNS trap:** "VMs in VNet-B can't resolve names in the private zone." → 📌 **The zone isn't LINKED to VNet-B.** Peering does **not** share DNS resolution — you need a **virtual network link** on the zone for **each** VNet.

⚠️ **Trap:** A VNet can have **only one** auto-registration zone link. If you need records in a second zone, create them manually or link without auto-registration.

### 📌 Custom DNS + on-prem resolution

| Scenario | Solution |
|---|---|
| VMs resolve Azure + internet | Default (`168.63.129.16`) |
| VMs must resolve **on-prem** names | Set **custom DNS servers** on the VNet → your DCs |
| **On-prem** must resolve Azure **private zones** | 📌 **Azure DNS Private Resolver — inbound endpoint** (or a DNS forwarder VM) |
| Azure must resolve **on-prem** and still use private zones | 📌 **Private Resolver — outbound endpoint + forwarding ruleset** |
| Both directions | Private Resolver with both endpoints |

📌 **Azure DNS Private Resolver is the modern answer.** It replaces "build a DNS forwarder VM," which is the legacy answer the exam may still show as a distractor.

⚠️ **Trap:** If you set **custom DNS servers** on a VNet, those servers must be able to forward to `168.63.129.16` for Azure private zone and Azure service resolution.
⚠️ **Trap:** Changing a VNet's DNS servers requires **restarting the VMs** (or renewing the DHCP lease) to take effect.

### Commands

```bash
# Public zone
az network dns zone create -g rg -n contoso.com
az network dns zone show -g rg -n contoso.com --query nameServers

az network dns record-set a add-record -g rg -z contoso.com -n www -a 203.0.113.5
az network dns record-set cname set-record -g rg -z contoso.com -n blog -c myapp.azurewebsites.net
az network dns record-set txt add-record -g rg -z contoso.com -n @ -v "v=spf1 include:spf.protection.outlook.com -all"
az network dns record-set mx add-record -g rg -z contoso.com -n @ -e mail.contoso.com -p 10
az network dns record-set a update -g rg -z contoso.com -n www --set ttl=300

# ALIAS record at the apex → public IP
az network dns record-set a create -g rg -z contoso.com -n @ \
  --target-resource <publicIpResourceId>

# ALIAS → Traffic Manager
az network dns record-set a create -g rg -z contoso.com -n www \
  --target-resource <trafficManagerProfileId>

# Private zone
az network private-dns zone create -g rg -n contoso.internal

az network private-dns link vnet create -g rg -n link-vnet1 \
  -z contoso.internal -v vnet1 -e true      # -e true = AUTO-REGISTRATION

az network private-dns link vnet create -g rg -n link-vnet2 \
  -z contoso.internal -v vnet2 -e false     # resolution only

az network private-dns record-set a add-record -g rg -z contoso.internal -n db -a 10.0.3.10
az network private-dns link vnet list -g rg -z contoso.internal -o table

# Custom DNS on a VNet
az network vnet update -g rg -n vnet1 --dns-servers 10.0.1.4 10.0.1.5
az network vnet update -g rg -n vnet1 --dns-servers ""     # revert to Azure DNS

# DNS Private Resolver
az dns-resolver create -g rg -n dnspr --id <vnetId> -l eastus
az dns-resolver inbound-endpoint create -g rg --dns-resolver-name dnspr -n inbound \
  --ip-configurations '[{"private-ip-allocation-method":"Dynamic","id":"<subnetId>"}]'
az dns-resolver outbound-endpoint create -g rg --dns-resolver-name dnspr -n outbound --id <subnetId>
```

---

## 5.8 Private Endpoints and Service Endpoints

> Covered in depth for storage in §3.10. This section adds the network-domain framing.

### 📌 The decision table

| Requirement | Answer |
|---|---|
| Free, simple, lock a PaaS service to a subnet | **Service endpoint** |
| Access from **on-premises** (VPN/ExpressRoute) | 📌 **Private endpoint** |
| Access from a **peered VNet** | 📌 **Private endpoint** |
| Give the PaaS resource a **private IP** | 📌 **Private endpoint** |
| Restrict to **one specific resource** (not the whole service) | 📌 **Private endpoint** |
| Prevent **data exfiltration** to other accounts in the service | 📌 **Private endpoint** |
| No DNS changes wanted | **Service endpoint** |
| Cheapest | **Service endpoint** (free) |

### Key extra terms

| Term | Definition |
|---|---|
| **Private Link service** | Publish **your own** service (behind a Standard Load Balancer) for consumers to reach via private endpoints. |
| **Private endpoint connection** | The approval workflow between consumer and provider (auto-approved if you own both). |
| **Group ID / sub-resource** | Which part of the service the private endpoint targets: `blob`, `file`, `sqlServer`, `sites`, `vault`, `registry`, etc. |
| **Service endpoint policy** | Restricts which **specific storage accounts** a service-endpoint-enabled subnet may reach — mitigates exfiltration. |
| **NSG with private endpoints** | ⚠️ NSGs on the private endpoint's subnet are supported (**with `--disable-private-endpoint-network-policies false`**); historically they were bypassed. |

⚠️ **Trap:** A private endpoint's NIC does **not** appear as a normal NIC you can attach an NSG to. Network policies must be **enabled** on the subnet for NSGs/UDRs to apply to private endpoint traffic.

⚠️ **Trap:** "Private endpoint created, connection shows Approved, but the app times out." → **DNS.** The name still resolves to the public IP. Link the `privatelink.*` private DNS zone to the VNet.

---

## 5.9 Azure Load Balancer

### Key Terms

| Term | Definition |
|---|---|
| **Azure Load Balancer** | 📌 **Layer 4** (TCP/UDP) load balancer. Ultra-low latency, high throughput, **regional**. |
| **Public LB** | Frontend = public IP. Load-balances internet traffic to backend VMs. Also provides **outbound SNAT**. |
| **Internal LB (ILB)** | Frontend = private IP. Load-balances internal traffic (e.g., web tier → app tier). |
| **Frontend IP configuration** | The IP clients connect to. |
| **Backend pool** | The NICs/IPs receiving traffic. |
| **Health probe** | Determines backend health. **Unhealthy instances receive no new traffic.** |
| **Load-balancing rule** | Maps frontend IP:port → backend pool:port, with a probe and session persistence. |
| **Inbound NAT rule** | Maps a frontend port to **one specific VM's** port (e.g., 50001 → VM1:3389). For management access. |
| **Outbound rule** | Explicit SNAT configuration (Standard LB only). |
| **Session persistence / distribution mode** | `None` (5-tuple, default), `Client IP` (2-tuple), `Client IP and protocol` (3-tuple). |
| **5-tuple hash** | src IP + src port + dest IP + dest port + protocol → picks the backend. |
| **HA Ports** | Standard **internal** LB rule load-balancing **all ports** at once — for NVAs. |
| **Floating IP / Direct Server Return (DSR)** | The backend sees the original destination IP. For SQL AlwaysOn listeners. |
| **SNAT port exhaustion** | ⚠️ Running out of outbound ports. The classic Standard LB outbound problem. |

### 📌 Basic vs Standard Load Balancer

| | Basic (⚠️ **retired 30 Sept 2025**) | **Standard** |
|---|---|---|
| Backend pool size | 300 | **1,000** |
| Backend endpoints | Single availability set / VMSS | **Any VM in the VNet** |
| **Availability Zones** | ❌ | ✅ **Zonal + zone-redundant** |
| **HA Ports** | ❌ | ✅ |
| Health probes | TCP, HTTP | TCP, HTTP, **HTTPS** |
| **Secure by default** | ❌ Open | ✅ **Closed — NSG required** |
| Outbound rules | ❌ Implicit | ✅ **Explicit + configurable** |
| **SLA** | ❌ **None** | ✅ **99.99%** |
| Diagnostics | Limited | Full (multi-dimensional metrics) |
| Cost | Free | Charged |
| Global tier | ❌ | ✅ **Cross-region LB** |

⚠️ **Trap:** **Basic LB has NO SLA.** If the question mentions an SLA, it's Standard.
⚠️ **Trap:** **Standard LB is closed by default** — you **must** have an NSG allowing the traffic. Basic is open.
⚠️ **Trap:** SKUs **cannot be mixed**. Standard LB requires **Standard** public IPs. A Basic PIP on a backend VM behind a Standard LB is invalid.

### 📌 Health probes — a heavily tested area

| Probe type | Checks | Success condition |
|---|---|---|
| **TCP** | TCP handshake on a port | Connection established |
| **HTTP** | GET to a path | 📌 **HTTP 200** response |
| **HTTPS** | GET to a path over TLS | HTTP 200 (Standard only) |

| Setting | Default | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **Interval** | **5 seconds** | How often to probe |
| **Unhealthy threshold** | **2** consecutive failures | Then the instance is out of rotation |
| Time to mark down | 📌 **~10 seconds** (5 s × 2) | |
| Probe source IP | 📌 **`168.63.129.16`** | The `AzureLoadBalancer` service tag |
| Port | Any | Often differs from the traffic port |

⚠️ **THE health probe trap:** 📌 The probe comes from **`168.63.129.16`**. If your NSG blocks the **`AzureLoadBalancer`** service tag inbound, **all probes fail, all backends are marked unhealthy, and the LB drops all traffic.** The default rule 65001 allows it — don't override it with a Deny.

⚠️ **Trap:** "The health probe returns HTTP 301/302/404 and the backend is unhealthy." → Only **HTTP 200** counts as healthy. Point the probe at a dedicated `/health` endpoint returning 200.

⚠️ **Trap:** "The backend VM's local firewall (Windows Firewall / iptables) blocks the probe port." → Backend unhealthy. The NSG being correct is not enough — the guest OS firewall must allow it too.

### 📌 Distribution modes

| Mode | Tuple | Behavior |
|---|---|---|
| **None (hash-based)** | **5-tuple** (src IP, src port, dst IP, dst port, protocol) | 📌 **Default.** Best distribution. Each new connection may hit a different backend. |
| **Client IP** | **2-tuple** (src IP, dst IP) | 📌 **Source IP affinity** — same client always hits the same backend. For **stateful** apps. |
| **Client IP and protocol** | **3-tuple** (src IP, dst IP, protocol) | Same, per protocol. |

⚠️ **Trap:** "Users are losing their shopping cart / session." → The app is **stateful** and default 5-tuple distribution sends them to different servers. → **Set session persistence to `Client IP`** (or, better, externalize session state to Redis).

### 📌 SNAT port exhaustion

When backend VMs have **no public IP** and reach the internet through the LB, they share the LB's frontend public IPs for **outbound SNAT**.

| Fact | Detail |
|---|---|
| Ports per frontend IP | **64,000** |
| Default allocation | Depends on pool size (e.g., 2–50 VMs → **1,024 ports each**) |
| Symptom | ⚠️ Intermittent outbound connection failures/timeouts under load |
| **Fix 1** 📌 | **NAT Gateway** — the recommended solution (scales SNAT automatically, up to 64k ports per PIP, 16 PIPs) |
| **Fix 2** | Add more **frontend public IPs** to the LB / use a **public IP prefix** |
| **Fix 3** | Configure **outbound rules** with a higher port allocation |
| **Fix 4** | Give each VM its **own public IP** (ILPIP) — wasteful |
| **Fix 5** | Use **connection pooling / keep-alives** in the app |
| **Fix 6** | Use **Private Endpoints** / service endpoints for Azure PaaS (avoids SNAT entirely) |

📌 **NAT Gateway is the modern, exam-correct answer to SNAT exhaustion.**

### 📌 Outbound connectivity methods (in Azure's priority order)

```
1. Public IP directly on the VM's NIC (ILPIP)     ← highest priority
2. NAT Gateway on the subnet                       ← RECOMMENDED
3. Load Balancer outbound rule
4. Default outbound access (⚠️ being retired 30 Sept 2025)
```

📌 If a subnet has a **NAT Gateway**, it **overrides** the LB's outbound rules for that subnet.

### Commands

```bash
# Standard public LB
az network lb create -g rg -n lb-web --sku Standard \
  --public-ip-address pip-lb --frontend-ip-name fe-web --backend-pool-name bepool

# Health probe
az network lb probe create -g rg --lb-name lb-web -n probe-http \
  --protocol Http --port 80 --path /health --interval 5 --threshold 2

# LB rule
az network lb rule create -g rg --lb-name lb-web -n rule-http \
  --protocol Tcp --frontend-port 80 --backend-port 80 \
  --frontend-ip-name fe-web --backend-pool-name bepool --probe-name probe-http \
  --idle-timeout 4 --enable-tcp-reset true --disable-outbound-snat true

# Session persistence
az network lb rule update -g rg --lb-name lb-web -n rule-http --load-distribution SourceIP

# Add backends
az network nic ip-config address-pool add -g rg --nic-name nic-web-01 \
  --ip-config-name ipconfig1 --lb-name lb-web --address-pool bepool

# Inbound NAT rule (RDP to a specific VM)
az network lb inbound-nat-rule create -g rg --lb-name lb-web -n rdp-vm1 \
  --protocol Tcp --frontend-port 50001 --backend-port 3389 --frontend-ip-name fe-web

# Internal LB
az network lb create -g rg -n lb-internal --sku Standard \
  --vnet-name vnet1 --subnet app \
  --private-ip-address 10.0.2.100 --frontend-ip-name fe-int --backend-pool-name bepool-app

# HA Ports (internal, Standard only) — for NVAs
az network lb rule create -g rg --lb-name lb-internal -n ha-ports \
  --protocol All --frontend-port 0 --backend-port 0 \
  --frontend-ip-name fe-int --backend-pool-name bepool-app --probe-name probe-tcp

# Outbound rule
az network lb outbound-rule create -g rg --lb-name lb-web -n outbound \
  --frontend-ip-configs fe-web --protocol All --address-pool bepool \
  --allocated-outbound-ports 10000 --idle-timeout 15

az network lb list -g rg -o table
az network lb show -g rg -n lb-web
```

📌 **`--frontend-port 0 --backend-port 0 --protocol All`** = **HA Ports**.

---

## 5.10 Application Gateway

### Key Terms

| Term | Definition |
|---|---|
| **Application Gateway (App GW)** | 📌 **Layer 7** (HTTP/HTTPS) load balancer. **Regional.** |
| **Listener** | Watches a frontend IP:port for incoming requests. **Basic** (port-based) or **Multi-site** (host-header-based). |
| **Rule** | Ties a listener to a backend pool / redirect. **Basic** or **Path-based**. |
| **Backend pool** | VMs, VMSS, IPs/FQDNs, **App Service**. |
| **HTTP settings / Backend settings** | Port, protocol, timeout, probe, cookie affinity, host-name override. |
| **Health probe** | Default (`/`) or custom. |
| **URL path-based routing** | 📌 Route `/images/*` → pool A, `/video/*` → pool B. |
| **Multi-site hosting** | 📌 Route by **host header**: `contoso.com` → pool A, `fabrikam.com` → pool B. |
| **SSL/TLS termination (offload)** | 📌 Decrypt at the gateway; backend gets plain HTTP. Reduces backend CPU. |
| **End-to-end TLS** | Re-encrypt to the backend. Needs the backend cert whitelisted (v1) / trusted root (v2). |
| **Cookie-based affinity** | 📌 `ApplicationGatewayAffinity` cookie pins a client to a backend. For stateful apps. |
| **Redirection** | HTTP → HTTPS, or to another listener/URL. |
| **Rewrite** | Modify HTTP headers and **URLs** (v2 only). |
| **Custom error pages** | Your own 403/502 pages. |
| **WAF (Web Application Firewall)** | 📌 **OWASP Core Rule Set** protection against SQLi, XSS, etc. |
| **WAF mode: Detection** | **Log only** — no blocking. |
| **WAF mode: Prevention** | **Block** malicious requests. |
| **WAF policy** | The reusable rule configuration (rule sets, exclusions, custom rules). |
| **Autoscaling** | v2 only — scales instances automatically. |
| **Capacity Unit (CU)** | v2 billing unit: compute + persistent connections + throughput. |
| **Instance count** | v1 sizing. |

### 📌 App GW v1 vs v2

| | v1 (⚠️ retired) | **v2 (Standard_v2 / WAF_v2)** |
|---|---|---|
| **Autoscaling** | ❌ | ✅ |
| **Zone redundancy** | ❌ | ✅ |
| **Static VIP** | ❌ (dynamic) | ✅ **Always static** |
| **Header/URL rewrite** | Headers only | ✅ **Headers + URL** |
| Performance | Baseline | **~5× faster** provisioning/scaling |
| Key Vault cert integration | ❌ | ✅ |
| Private Link | ❌ | ✅ |
| **Public IP SKU** | Basic/Standard | **Standard, static required** |
| Status | Retired | 📌 Current |

### 📌 App GW requirements — exam traps

| Requirement | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Dedicated subnet** | 📌 App GW needs its **own subnet** — nothing else in it |
| Subnet size | `/24` recommended; `/26` minimum for v2 |
| Public IP | 📌 **v2 requires a Standard, STATIC public IP** |
| One App GW per subnet | Multiple gateways can share a subnet only if all are the same version |
| NSG on the subnet | 📌 Must allow inbound **`GatewayManager`** on **65200–65535** (v2) or 65503–65534 (v1), and **`AzureLoadBalancer`** |
| Region | Regional (not global) |

⚠️ **THE App GW trap:** 📌 **You must allow inbound TCP 65200–65535 from the `GatewayManager` service tag** on the App GW subnet's NSG. Block it → the gateway shows **Unhealthy** and cannot be managed. This is asked often.

### 📌 Routing decisions

| Requirement | Feature |
|---|---|
| `contoso.com/images/*` → image servers, `/video/*` → video servers | 📌 **URL path-based routing** (path-based rule) |
| `contoso.com` → pool A, `fabrikam.com` → pool B on one gateway | 📌 **Multi-site listener** (host header) |
| Force all HTTP to HTTPS | 📌 **Redirection rule** (HTTP listener → redirect to the HTTPS listener) |
| Offload TLS to save backend CPU | 📌 **TLS termination** |
| Compliance requires encryption all the way | 📌 **End-to-end TLS** |
| Block SQL injection | 📌 **WAF_v2 in Prevention mode** |
| Session stickiness | 📌 **Cookie-based affinity** |
| Rewrite `X-Forwarded-For` or strip a header | 📌 **Rewrite rule (v2)** |

### 📌 WAF

| Fact | Detail |
|---|---|
| Rule sets | **OWASP CRS 3.2 / 3.1 / 3.0**, and **Microsoft Default Rule Set (DRS) 2.x** |
| Modes | **Detection** (log only) / **Prevention** (block) |
| Default max request body size | **128 KB** (inspection limit) |
| File upload limit | 100 MB (v2) |
| Exclusions | Skip inspection for specific headers/cookies/args (to fix false positives) |
| Custom rules | Match on IP, geo, size, string → Allow/Block/Log |
| Bot protection | Managed bot rule set |
| Where else is WAF available? | **Azure Front Door** (global) and **Azure CDN** |

⚠️ **Trap:** "WAF is blocking legitimate traffic." → Switch to **Detection** mode to observe, then add **exclusions** or disable the specific rule ID. Don't just turn WAF off.
⚠️ **Trap:** "New WAF deployment, want to see impact without breaking anything." → **Detection mode** first.

### Commands

```bash
az network application-gateway create -g rg -n appgw-web \
  --sku WAF_v2 --capacity 2 \
  --vnet-name vnet1 --subnet appgw-subnet \
  --public-ip-address pip-appgw \
  --http-settings-protocol Http --http-settings-port 80 \
  --frontend-port 443 --servers 10.0.1.4 10.0.1.5 \
  --cert-file cert.pfx --cert-password <pw> \
  --priority 100

# Autoscale (v2)
az network application-gateway update -g rg -n appgw-web \
  --set autoscaleConfiguration.minCapacity=2 autoscaleConfiguration.maxCapacity=10 \
  --set sku.capacity=null

# Backend pools
az network application-gateway address-pool create -g rg --gateway-name appgw-web \
  -n pool-images --servers 10.0.1.10 10.0.1.11
az network application-gateway address-pool create -g rg --gateway-name appgw-web \
  -n pool-video --servers 10.0.1.20 10.0.1.21

# Path map (URL path-based routing)
az network application-gateway url-path-map create -g rg --gateway-name appgw-web \
  -n pathmap --paths "/images/*" --address-pool pool-images \
  --default-address-pool pool-video --http-settings http-settings

az network application-gateway url-path-map rule create -g rg --gateway-name appgw-web \
  --path-map-name pathmap -n video-rule --paths "/video/*" --address-pool pool-video

# Multi-site listener
az network application-gateway http-listener create -g rg --gateway-name appgw-web \
  -n listener-contoso --frontend-port port443 --host-name www.contoso.com --ssl-cert cert1

# HTTP → HTTPS redirect
az network application-gateway redirect-config create -g rg --gateway-name appgw-web \
  -n http-to-https --type Permanent --target-listener listener-https \
  --include-path true --include-query-string true

# Health probe
az network application-gateway probe create -g rg --gateway-name appgw-web \
  -n probe-health --protocol Http --host-name-from-http-settings true \
  --path /health --interval 30 --timeout 30 --threshold 3

# WAF policy
az network application-gateway waf-policy create -g rg -n waf-policy
az network application-gateway waf-policy policy-setting update -g rg \
  --policy-name waf-policy --mode Prevention --state Enabled \
  --max-request-body-size-in-kb 128

az network application-gateway waf-policy managed-rule rule-set add -g rg \
  --policy-name waf-policy --type OWASP --version 3.2

az network application-gateway show-backend-health -g rg -n appgw-web
```

📌 **`show-backend-health`** is the App GW troubleshooting command — the equivalent of "effective rules" for NSGs.

---

## 5.11 Comparing Load Balancing Services

**This comparison is asked on virtually every AZ-104 exam. Learn the 2×2.**

### 📌 The master table

| | **Load Balancer** | **Application Gateway** | **Traffic Manager** | **Front Door** |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **OSI Layer** | **L4** (TCP/UDP) | **L7** (HTTP/S) | **DNS** (L7-ish) | **L7** (HTTP/S) |
| **Scope** | 📌 **Regional** | 📌 **Regional** | 📌 **Global** | 📌 **Global** |
| Protocol | Any TCP/UDP | HTTP, HTTPS, HTTP/2, WebSocket | Any (DNS-based) | HTTP, HTTPS |
| Traffic path | Through the LB | Through the gateway | 📌 **NOT in the path** — DNS only | Through the Microsoft edge |
| **WAF** | ❌ | ✅ | ❌ | ✅ |
| **SSL offload** | ❌ | ✅ | ❌ | ✅ |
| **URL path routing** | ❌ | ✅ | ❌ | ✅ |
| **Cookie affinity** | Source IP only | ✅ Cookie | ❌ | ✅ Cookie |
| **Caching / CDN** | ❌ | ❌ | ❌ | ✅ |
| **Anycast** | ❌ | ❌ | ❌ | ✅ |
| Health probes | TCP/HTTP/S | HTTP/S | HTTP/S/TCP | HTTP/S |
| Failover speed | Seconds | Seconds | ⚠️ **DNS TTL-bound (minutes)** | Seconds |
| Zone redundant | ✅ (Standard) | ✅ (v2) | Global | Global |
| Use for | VMs, non-HTTP, internal tiers | Regional web apps + WAF | Global DNS routing, non-HTTP global | Global web apps + WAF + CDN |

### 📌 The 2×2 decision matrix

|  | **Regional** | **Global** |
|---|---|---|
| **HTTP(S) — Layer 7** | 📌 **Application Gateway** | 📌 **Front Door** |
| **Non-HTTP — Layer 4 / DNS** | 📌 **Load Balancer** | 📌 **Traffic Manager** |

💡 **The single most useful thing to memorize in the networking domain.**

### 📌 Traffic Manager routing methods

| Method | Sends traffic to | Use for |
|---|---|---|
| **Priority** | The highest-priority healthy endpoint | 📌 **Active/passive failover / DR** |
| **Weighted** | Endpoints by assigned weight | 📌 **A/B testing, canary, gradual migration** |
| **Performance** | The endpoint with the **lowest latency** to the client | 📌 **Global apps, best user experience** |
| **Geographic** | Based on the **user's geographic origin** | 📌 **Data sovereignty / compliance / GDPR / localized content** |
| **Multivalue** | Returns **multiple** healthy IPs in one response | Client-side retry; **IPv4/IPv6 endpoints only** |
| **Subnet** | Based on the client's **source IP range** | Different content for internal vs external users |

⚠️ **Trap:** "EU users' data must stay in the EU." → **Geographic** routing (not Performance — Performance picks by latency and could send a Paris user to a US endpoint if it's faster).
⚠️ **Trap:** "Send 10% of traffic to the new version." → **Weighted**.
⚠️ **Trap:** "Fail over to the secondary region if the primary is down." → **Priority**.
⚠️ **Trap:** "Lowest latency for a global audience." → **Performance**.

### 📌 The Traffic Manager traps

1. 📌 **Traffic Manager is DNS-based — it is NOT in the data path.** It just returns a CNAME/IP. The client connects directly.
2. 📌 **Failover is limited by DNS TTL.** A 300 s TTL means up to 5 minutes of failure before clients move. Lower the TTL for faster failover (default is 60 s in TM).
3. **Clients cache DNS.** Some ignore TTL entirely. This is why TM is not "instant failover."
4. **Traffic Manager cannot do SSL offload, caching, or WAF.** It's just DNS.
5. **Nested profiles** allow combining methods (e.g., Geographic at the top, Performance beneath).

### 📌 Front Door tiers

| | Standard | Premium |
|---|---|---|
| CDN caching | ✅ | ✅ |
| Custom domains + free managed certs | ✅ | ✅ |
| Rules engine | ✅ | ✅ |
| **WAF (managed rule sets)** | ⚠️ Custom rules only | ✅ **Managed + bot protection** |
| **Private Link to origin** | ❌ | ✅ |
| Bot protection | ❌ | ✅ |

### 📌 Common combinations

| Architecture | Stack |
|---|---|
| Global web app with WAF | **Front Door (WAF) → regional App Gateways → VMSS** |
| or | **Front Door → App Service (multiple regions)** |
| Regional web app | **App Gateway (WAF_v2) → VMSS** |
| Internal 3-tier | **App GW (public) → web VMs → Internal LB → app VMs → Internal LB → DB VMs** |
| Global non-HTTP (e.g., game servers, SMTP) | **Traffic Manager → regional Standard Load Balancers** |
| Global HTTP, need caching | **Front Door** |

⚠️ **Trap:** "Front Door and Application Gateway together — is that redundant?" → **No.** Front Door = global entry + CDN + global WAF; App GW = regional L7 + path routing. A very common enterprise pattern. Lock the App GW down to the **`AzureFrontDoor.Backend`** service tag so nobody bypasses Front Door.

---

## 5.12 Network Watcher

### Key Terms

| Term | Definition |
|---|---|
| **Network Watcher** | Regional monitoring/diagnostic toolset for Azure networking. **Auto-enabled per region** when you create a VNet. |
| **IP Flow Verify** | 📌 "Is this 5-tuple allowed or denied — **and by which NSG rule**?" |
| **NSG Diagnostics** | Newer, richer version of IP Flow Verify showing the full rule evaluation. |
| **Next Hop** | 📌 "Where does a packet to X go?" Returns the next hop type + IP + the **route ID** that decided it. |
| **Effective security rules** | The merged subnet+NIC NSG rules on a NIC. |
| **Connection Troubleshoot** | 📌 One-off end-to-end connectivity test between two endpoints with hop-by-hop latency and the blocking reason. |
| **Connection Monitor** | 📌 **Continuous**, scheduled connectivity monitoring with alerts. Supports Azure + hybrid + external endpoints. |
| **VPN Troubleshoot** | Diagnose VPN gateways and connections. |
| **Packet Capture** | 📌 Capture packets on a VM (needs the **Network Watcher extension**). Saves to storage or the VM's disk. |
| **NSG Flow Logs** | 📌 Log every allowed/denied flow to a storage account. **v2** adds byte/packet counts. ⚠️ Being replaced by **VNet Flow Logs**. |
| **VNet Flow Logs** | Successor to NSG flow logs — logged at the **VNet** level. |
| **Traffic Analytics** | 📌 Analyzes flow logs in **Log Analytics** — visualizes top talkers, malicious flows, geo maps. |
| **Topology** | Visual map of the VNet's resources. |
| **Connection Monitor test group** | Sources + destinations + test configuration. |

### 📌 The tool-selection table — heavily tested

| Question | Tool |
|---|---|
| "Which **NSG rule** is blocking this traffic?" | 📌 **IP Flow Verify** (or NSG Diagnostics) |
| "Where does traffic to `10.2.0.5` go?" | 📌 **Next Hop** |
| "Which rules apply to this NIC?" | 📌 **Effective security rules** |
| "Test connectivity from VM1 to VM2 **right now**" | 📌 **Connection Troubleshoot** |
| "Monitor connectivity from VM1 to VM2 **continuously** and alert" | 📌 **Connection Monitor** |
| "Capture the actual packets for deep analysis" | 📌 **Packet Capture** |
| "Log all traffic for a security audit / see who talked to whom" | 📌 **NSG/VNet Flow Logs** |
| "Visualize traffic patterns and find malicious flows" | 📌 **Traffic Analytics** |
| "Diagnose why my site-to-site VPN is down" | 📌 **VPN Troubleshoot** |
| "See a diagram of my VNet" | 📌 **Topology** |

⚠️ **THE Network Watcher trap:** **IP Flow Verify** vs **Connection Troubleshoot**:
- **IP Flow Verify** = *NSG rules only*. Tells you allow/deny + the rule name. Doesn't test the actual connection.
- **Connection Troubleshoot** = *actually tries the connection* and reports NSG, UDR, DNS, or in-guest issues.
- **Connection Monitor** = the *continuous* version of Connection Troubleshoot.

⚠️ **Trap:** Packet Capture **requires the Network Watcher agent extension** on the VM. It also requires the VM to be **running**.

### 📌 NSG Flow Logs

| Fact | Detail |
|---|---|
| Destination | 📌 **A storage account** (required) — not a Log Analytics workspace directly |
| Traffic Analytics | Reads from storage → writes to **Log Analytics** |
| Version 1 | 5-tuple + allow/deny |
| **Version 2** | 📌 Adds **flow state** (Begin/Continuing/End) and **byte/packet counts** |
| Retention | 0 (forever) to 365 days |
| Format | JSON |
| Log Analytics query table | `AzureNetworkAnalytics_CL` |
| Cost | Storage + Traffic Analytics processing |

⚠️ **Trap:** "NSG flow logs go to Log Analytics." → **No.** They go to a **storage account**. **Traffic Analytics** is the feature that then pulls them into Log Analytics.

### Commands

```bash
az network watcher configure -g NetworkWatcherRG -l eastus --enabled true

# IP Flow Verify
az network watcher test-ip-flow -g rg --vm vm-web-01 \
  --direction Inbound --protocol TCP \
  --local 10.0.1.4:443 --remote 203.0.113.5:60000 \
  --nic nic-web-01

# Next hop
az network watcher show-next-hop -g rg --vm vm-web-01 \
  --source-ip 10.0.1.4 --dest-ip 10.2.0.5

# Effective rules / routes
az network nic list-effective-nsg -g rg -n nic-web-01
az network nic show-effective-route-table -g rg -n nic-web-01 -o table

# Connection troubleshoot
az network watcher test-connectivity -g rg --source-resource vm-web-01 \
  --dest-address www.microsoft.com --dest-port 443

az network watcher test-connectivity -g rg --source-resource vm-web-01 \
  --dest-resource vm-db-01 --dest-port 1433

# Connection Monitor (continuous)
az network watcher connection-monitor create -g rg -n cm-web-db \
  --location eastus --endpoint-source-name src --endpoint-source-resource-id <vmId> \
  --endpoint-dest-name dst --endpoint-dest-address 10.0.3.10 \
  --test-config-name tc1 --protocol Tcp --tcp-port 1433 --frequency 30

# Packet capture
az network watcher packet-capture create -g rg --vm vm-web-01 -n capture1 \
  --storage-account stacct --time-limit 300 --file-size 100 \
  --filters '[{"protocol":"TCP","localPort":"443"}]'
az network watcher packet-capture show-status -n capture1 -l eastus
az network watcher packet-capture stop -n capture1 -l eastus

# Flow logs
az network watcher flow-log create -g rg -n flowlog-web -l eastus \
  --nsg nsg-web --storage-account stacct --retention 30 \
  --format JSON --log-version 2 \
  --workspace <logAnalyticsWorkspaceId> --traffic-analytics true --interval 10
```
